<label><sup>2</sup></label>Beijing Sankuai Online Technology Co., Ltd., Beijing, P. R. China</aff><author-notes><fn id="cor1"><label>✉</label><p>Address correspondence to Lingyan Zhu, College of Environmental Science and Engineering, Nankai University, 38 Tongyan Road, Jinnan District, Tianjin, P.R. China 300350. Email: <email>zhuly@nankai.edu.cn</email></p></fn><fn id="_fncrsp93pmc__"><label>✉</label><p>Corresponding author.</p></fn></author-notes><pub-date><day>13</day><month>12</month><year>2023</year></pub-date><volume>131</volume><issue>12</issue><fpage>127012</fpage><page-range>127012</page-range><pub-history><event event-type="pmc-release"><date><day>13</day><month>12</month><year>2023</year></date></event></pub-history><permissions><license><license-p><italic>EHP</italic> is an open-access journal published with support from the National Institute of Environmental Health Sciences, National Institutes of Health. All content is public domain unless otherwise noted.</license-p></license></permissions><self-uri xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="ehp11969.pdf" content-type="pmc-pdf"><?cloudpmc-path 4169/10718298/1a88117c99a1/ehp11969.pdf?><?cloudpmc-bucket app?><?size 1113144?></self-uri><abstract id="abstract1"><title>Abstract</title><sec id="sec1" disp-level="2"><title>Background:</title><p>Oral ingestion, inhalation, and skin contact are important exposure routes for humans to uptake per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS). However, nasal and dermal exposure to PFAS remains unclear, and accurately predicting internal body burden of PFAS in humans via multiple exposure pathways is urgently required.</p></sec><sec id="sec2" disp-level="2"><title>Objectives:</title><p>We aimed to develop multiple physiologically based toxicokinetic (PBTK) models to unveil the route-specific pharmacokinetics and bioavailability of PFAS via respective oral, nasal, and dermal exposure pathways using a mouse model and sought to predict the internal concentrations in various tissues through multiple exposure routes and extrapolate it to humans.</p></sec><sec id="sec3" disp-level="2"><title>Methods:</title><p>Mice were administered the mixed solution of perfluorohexane sulfonate, perfluorooctane sulfonate, and perfluorooctanoic acid through oral, nasal, and dermal exposure separately or jointly. The time-dependent concentrations of PFAS in plasma and tissues were determined to calibrate and validate the individual and combined PBTK models, which were applied in single- and repeated-dose scenarios.</p></sec><sec id="sec4" disp-level="2"><title>Results:</title><p>The developed route-specific PBTK models successfully simulated the tissue concentrations of PFAS in mice following single or joint exposure routes as well as long-term repeated dose scenarios. The time to peak concentration of PFAS in plasma via dermal exposure was much longer (34.1–83.0 h) than that via nasal exposure (0.960 h). The bioavailability of PFAS via oral exposure was the highest (73.2%–98.0%), followed by nasal (33.9%–66.8%) and dermal exposure (4.59%–7.80%). This model was extrapolated to predict internal levels in human under real environment.</p></sec><sec id="sec5" disp-level="2"><title>Discussion:</title><p>Based on these data, we predict the following: PFAS were absorbed quickly via nasal exposure, whereas a distinct hysteresis effect was observed for dermal exposure. Almost all the PFAS to which mice were exposed via gastrointestinal route were absorbed into plasma, which exhibited the highest bioavailability. Exhalation clearance greatly depressed the bioavailability of PFAS via nasal exposure, whereas the lowest bioavailability in dermal exposure was because of the interception of PFAS within the skin layers. <ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://doi.org/10.1289/EHP11969" ext-link-type="uri">https://doi.org/10.1289/EHP11969</ext-link></p></sec></abstract><custom-meta-group><custom-meta><meta-name>status</meta-name><meta-value>released</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>display-pdf</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>is-olf</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>is-manuscript</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>is-preprint</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>is-journal-matter</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>is-scanned</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>is-retracted</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta></custom-meta-group></article-meta><notes notes-type="article-notes"><sec id="fn-group1" sec-type="fn-group" disp-level="2"><fn-group><fn id="fn1"><p>Supplemental Material is available online (<ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://doi.org/10.1289/EHP11969" ext-link-type="uri">https://doi.org/10.1289/EHP11969</ext-link>).</p></fn><fn id="fn2"><p>The authors have no conflicts to declare.</p></fn><fn id="fn3"><p><bold>Note to readers with disabilities:</bold>

2022 Aug 4; Revised 2023 Sep 8; Accepted 2023 Nov 8; Collection date 2023 Dec.</p></sec></notes></front><body><sec id="s1" disp-level="1"><title>Introduction</title><p>Since the 1950s, per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) have been widely used in a variety of industrial and household fields owing to their excellent surface activity, thermal and chemical stability, and hydro- and oleophobic nature.<sup><xref rid="c1" ref-type="bibr">1</xref></sup> These qualities inevitably led to their ubiquitous occurrence and distribution in global ecosystems.<sup><xref rid="c2" ref-type="bibr">2</xref></sup> In particular, a growing body of evidence of toxic effects of some PFAS, such as perfluorooctanoic acid (PFOA) and perfluorooctane sulfonate (PFOS), prompt global regulations and limitations on them and on related compounds.<sup><xref rid="c3" ref-type="bibr">3</xref></sup> A couple of PFAS with chain length shorter than PFOA and PFOS, such as perfluorohexane sulfonate (PFHxS), have been mass-produced assuming their lower accumulation potential and toxicities.<sup><xref rid="c4" ref-type="bibr">4</xref></sup> However, it has been demonstrated that PFHxS tended to accumulate with a longer half-life than that of PFOS in humans and also exhibited harmful effects on human health.<sup><xref rid="c5" ref-type="bibr">5</xref>–<xref rid="c7" ref-type="bibr">7</xref></sup> Thus, PFHxS has been proposed for restriction under the Stockholm Convention.<sup><xref rid="c8" ref-type="bibr">8</xref></sup></p><p>Humans are exposed to persistent organic pollutants mainly through dietary consumption of food and water, inhalation of air, ingestion of particulate matter, and skin contact with dust, clothing, or cosmetics.<sup><xref rid="c9" ref-type="bibr">9</xref></sup> Epidemiological studies and exposure investigations reported that oral ingestion was the main exposure route of PFAS.<sup><xref rid="c10" ref-type="bibr">10</xref></sup> However, an increasing number of studies have shown that nasal and dermal exposure also made a distinct contribution to PFAS load in the human body, particularly in some special populations.<sup><xref rid="c9" ref-type="bibr">9</xref>,<xref rid="c11" ref-type="bibr">11</xref></sup> It was reported that exposure via respiration accounted for 82% and 77% of the total intake of PFOS and PFOA in children, respectively, in a dust-rich environment.<sup><xref rid="c11" ref-type="bibr">11</xref></sup> The detection of PFAS in masks gives another route for inhalation exposure.<sup><xref rid="c12" ref-type="bibr">12</xref></sup> Given the high levels of PFAS detected in cosmetics (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i1" display="inline" alttext="22 to 10,500 nanograms per gram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>22</mml:mn><mml:mo>–</mml:mo><mml:mn>10,500</mml:mn><mml:mtext> ng</mml:mtext><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>), dermal exposure might make a great contribution to the people who frequently apply cosmetics containing PFAS.<sup><xref rid="c13" ref-type="bibr">13</xref></sup></p><p>Associations between the environmental levels of PFAS and their concentrations in human serum have been observed.<sup><xref rid="c14" ref-type="bibr">14</xref>–<xref rid="c16" ref-type="bibr">16</xref></sup> For example, frequent seafood consumption was associated with elevated PFAS serum concentrations in Inuit people in Greenland,<sup><xref rid="c14" ref-type="bibr">14</xref></sup> whalers in the Faroe Islands,<sup><xref rid="c15" ref-type="bibr">15</xref></sup> and fishery employees in China.<sup><xref rid="c16" ref-type="bibr">16</xref></sup> A significant correlation was also found between the levels of PFOA in the outdoor activity hand-wipes and human serum.<sup><xref rid="c17" ref-type="bibr">17</xref></sup> High-level PFAS concentrations were observed in the serum of workers who often inhaled aerosols of PFAS.<sup><xref rid="c18" ref-type="bibr">18</xref></sup> However, up to now, there is sparse information on the quantitative relationship between route-specific external exposure levels and internal concentrations of PFAS. Compared with traditional compartment models, physiologically based toxicokinetic (PBTK) model can simulate the absorption, distribution, metabolism and elimination (ADME) of pollutants by properly including description of the physiological parameters of absorption sites and kinetic parameters of the absorption processes, providing a powerful tool to understand the pharmacokinetics via different exposure routes.<sup><xref rid="c19" ref-type="bibr">19</xref></sup> However, the present PBTK models are based on the assumption that PFAS are injected directly into the blood intravenously or only include gastrointestinal absorption of PFAS.<sup><xref rid="c20" ref-type="bibr">20</xref>,<xref rid="c21" ref-type="bibr">21</xref></sup> To our knowledge, no dermal or nasal PBTK model for PFAS has been developed, let alone been used in the comparison between different exposure routes. In addition, only PFOS or PFOA was selected as the target compound in most of the previous PBTK models without considering other PFAS such as PFHxS.<sup><xref rid="c22" ref-type="bibr">22</xref>–<xref rid="c24" ref-type="bibr">24</xref></sup></p><p>Only the proportion of a chemical that reaches the systemic circulation is considered to be bioavailable, which is generally <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i2" display="inline" alttext="less than 100 percent"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&lt;</mml:mo><mml:mn>100</mml:mn><mml:mo>%</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> for organic pollutants based on <italic>in vivo</italic> mouse model.<sup><xref rid="c25" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>,<xref rid="c26" ref-type="bibr">26</xref></sup> The bioavailability of a compound is dependent on the exposure routes because the compound undergoes different absorption processes such as digestion in gastrointestinal tract, skin interception, and pulmonary gas exchange.<sup><xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup> According to mouse and rat studies, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i3" display="inline" alttext="greater than 95 percent"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&gt;</mml:mo><mml:mn>95</mml:mn><mml:mo>%</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of orally administered PFAS are rapidly absorbed through oral exposure and primarily accumulate in plasma, liver, and kidney.<sup><xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref>–<xref rid="c29" ref-type="bibr">29</xref></sup> However, there is sparse information about the dermal or nasal exposure to PFAS. In a study in which rats received 6 h of nose-only exposure to PFOA-spiked aerosols, the time to reach the peak concentration (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i4" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t begin subscript maximum end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">T</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>max</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) of PFOA in male rat plasma was <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i5" display="inline" alttext="approximately 3"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>∼</mml:mo><mml:mtext> </mml:mtext><mml:mn>3</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> h after exposure,<sup><xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref></sup> which was even quicker than with a single oral exposure (10 h).<sup><xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup> To our knowledge, the bioavailability of PFAS following nasal exposure is unknown. For dermal exposure, one of our recent studies using an <italic>in vivo</italic> rat model observed that dermal penetration of PFAS in rats with <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i6" display="inline" alttext="uppercase c begin subscript uppercase f end subscript greater than 5"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">C</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">F</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&gt;</mml:mo><mml:mn>5</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> [including six perfluoroalkyl carboxylic acids (PFCA), two perfluorosulfonate acids (PFSA), and two polyfluoroalkyl phosphate diesters (diPAP)] lasted a long period with a <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i7" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t begin subscript maximum end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">T</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>max</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of 72 h.<sup><xref rid="c31" ref-type="bibr">31</xref></sup> It suggests that 4.1%–18.0% of the applied PFAS were absorbed into the rat blood 144 h after dermal dosing, which was dependent on their physicochemical properties.<sup><xref rid="c31" ref-type="bibr">31</xref></sup> Due to the few data and inconsistent exposure doses and durations applied in these studies, it is difficult to determine the influence of exposure routes on absorption kinetics, and in-depth studies are warranted. Most current risk assessment studies on PFAS via different exposure pathways were based on the external levels of PFAS in the exposure media, which might overestimate the exposure risks because the pharmacokinetics and bioavailability of PFAS were not considered.<sup><xref rid="c17" ref-type="bibr">17</xref>,<xref rid="c32" ref-type="bibr">32</xref></sup> It is imperative to develop models to accurately evaluate the internal exposure levels of PFAS by comprehensively considering the multiple exposure routes and the specific bioavailability.</p><p>In this study, PFOA, PFOS, and PFHxS were selected as target compounds, considering that they are highly detected in various environmental matrices, with various functional groups or chain lengths, recalcitrance for biotransformation,<sup><xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref>,<xref rid="c33" ref-type="bibr">33</xref></sup> which is conducive to tracking and comparing their <italic>in vivo</italic> pharmacokinetics. Single-dose exposure experiments were conducted following separate and joint oral, nasal, dermal, and intravenous administration. The uptake and elimination kinetics of PFAS via different routes and the route-specific bioavailability of PFAS were investigated systematically, which allowed us to calibrate and validate the route-specific PBTK models for PFAS. Then the established models were evaluated under repeated exposure scenarios derived from previous studies, and then we further extrapolated to humans. This study established a reliable platform to quantitatively describe the correlation between external exposure via different routes to internal body loads of PFAS.</p></sec><sec id="s2" disp-level="1"><title>Method and Materials</title><sec id="s2.1" disp-level="2"><title>Chemicals and Materials</title><p>The native and mass labeled standards of PFHxS, PFOS, PFOA were purchased from Wellington Laboratories (L-PFHxS, L-PFOS, L-PFOS, and MPFAS-MXA) (Table S1). Sodium hydroxide (NaOH, 96%) and tetrabutyl ammonium hydrogen sulfate (TBAHs) were obtained from Shanghai Macklin Biochemical Technology Co., Ltd. (S835850 and T819011). Methyl tert-butyl ether (MTBE) was obtained from Concord Science and Technology (8051).</p></sec><sec id="s2.2" disp-level="2"><title>Mice Treatment</title><p>Male CD-1 mice of <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i8" display="inline" alttext="33 plus or minus 3 grams"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>33</mml:mn><mml:mo>±</mml:mo><mml:mn>3</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> were purchased from Beijing Vital River Laboratory Animal Technology Co., and the animal experiments were approved by the Animal Ethics Committee of the Institute of Radiation Medicine, Chinese Academy of Medical Sciences (Approval Number: IRM-DWLL-2021189). The mice were housed in polypropylene mouse cages with a 12 h light/dark cycle and were given free access to regular mouse chow and water.</p><p>The mice were randomly assigned into nine groups after 1 wk of acclimatization (Table S2). They were dosed through separate exposure routes, including single gavage (SG), nasal drip (ND), and intradermal injection (ID; outer thigh skin) to simulate oral, nasal, and dermal exposures, respectively. ND introduces liquid into the nostrils, allowing the compound to pass through the respiratory tract autonomously into the lung. In brief, the mouse was held up with their nostrils pointing straight up, and then <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i9" display="inline" alttext="50 microliters"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>50</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of the mixed PFAS solution was dropped into one nostril through a syringe. To control the applied dosage accurately, ID, which bypasses the process of epidermal penetration, was used. The skin on the outer thigh was carefully shaved before injection. The needle was gently inserted into the skin for a few millimeters while being held virtually parallel to the skin’s surface. A bleb will develop following an effective injection. To calculate the bioavailability of PFAS via different exposure routes, a tail intravenous injection (IV) group was set up to calibrate related parameters of the PBTK models (discussed later). The mice were fasted overnight before administration. Mixed PFAS solutions of PFHxS, PFOS, and PFOA at equal concentrations were prepared. Low (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i10" display="inline" alttext="1 milligram per kilogram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> body weight for each PFAS; Table S2, Groups 1–4) and high dosage levels (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i11" display="inline" alttext="5 milligrams per kilogram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>5</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> body weight for each PFAS; Table S2, Groups 5–8) and a blank control consisting of 0.5% of Tween-20 in pure Milli-Q water were set up for each exposure route, and all treatments were conducted with four replicates (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i12" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic n equals 4"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>4</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>). Following the guidance for administered volume,<sup><xref rid="c34" ref-type="bibr">34</xref>–<xref rid="c36" ref-type="bibr">36</xref></sup> the mice in the SG, ND, ID, and IV groups were dosed with <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i13" display="inline" alttext="330 microliters"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>330</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i14" display="inline" alttext="100 grams per liter"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>100</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">g</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> (or <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i15" display="inline" alttext="500 grams per liter"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>500</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">g</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>), <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i16" display="inline" alttext="50 microliters"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>50</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i17" display="inline" alttext="660 grams per liter"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>660</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">g</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> (or <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i18" display="inline" alttext="1,320 grams per liter"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1,320</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">g</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>), <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i19" display="inline" alttext="50 microliters"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>50</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i20" display="inline" alttext="660 grams per liter"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>660</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">g</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> (or <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i21" display="inline" alttext="1,320 grams per liter"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1,320</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">g</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>), and <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i22" display="inline" alttext="100 microliters"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>100</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i23" display="inline" alttext="330 grams per liter"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>330</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">g</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> (or <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i24" display="inline" alttext="1650 grams per liter"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1,650</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">g</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) the mixed solutions, respectively, for the low-level (or high-level) dosed groups so that the dosages in the mice were similar among the different route exposure groups. On 4 h, 16 h, and days 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, and 32 post dosage, the mice were euthanized through cervical dislocation to collect plasma (from the orbital vein), liver, kidney, lung, and skin of the outer thighs (only sampled in orally, nasally, and intravenously dosed groups). The collected tissue was immediately stored at <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i25" display="inline" alttext="negative 20 degrees Celsius"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:mn>20</mml:mn><mml:mo>°</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">C</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> before analysis.</p><p>To simulate the realistic scenario where organisms and humans are usually exposed to PFAS via multiple exposure pathways simultaneously, another group of mice was exposed to PFAS through the three routes concomitantly, each at a dosage of <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i26" display="inline" alttext="1 milligram per kilogram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> body weight (Table S2, Group 9) (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i27" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic n equals 4"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>4</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>). Plasma, liver, and kidney were sampled on days 2, 16, and 32 and processed following the procedure described above.</p></sec><sec id="s2.3" disp-level="2"><title>Extraction and Instrumental Analysis</title><p>The mice livers, kidneys, and lungs were homogenized with phosphate-buffered saline (PBS) by a Freezer Mixer (1:3, 1:1, 1:2 wt/vol, respectively). The mouse skin was cut into pieces with anatomical scissors. Before sample extraction, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i28" display="inline" alttext="1 nanogram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mtext> ng</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of isotopically labeled standards were added to a <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i29" display="inline" alttext="15 milliliters"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>15</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> polypropylene tube that contained the homogenized tissue samples (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i30" display="inline" alttext="10 microliters"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>10</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of plasma, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i31" display="inline" alttext="10 microliters"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>10</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of liver homogenate, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i32" display="inline" alttext="20 microliters"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>20</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of kidney homogenate, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i33" display="inline" alttext="50 microliters"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>50</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of lung homogenate, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i34" display="inline" alttext="100 milligrams"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>100</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of skin, respectively).<sup><xref rid="c37" ref-type="bibr">37</xref></sup> Next, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i35" display="inline" alttext="1 milliliter"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of 0.5 M TBAH (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i36" display="inline" alttext="potential of hydrogen equals 10"><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>pH</mml:mtext><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>10</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>), <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i37" display="inline" alttext="2 milliliters"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i38" display="inline" alttext="0.25 molar"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>0.25</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">M</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> sodium carbonate buffer, and <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i39" display="inline" alttext="5 milliliters"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>5</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of MTBE were added and then shaken at <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i40" display="inline" alttext="250 revolutions per minute"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>250</mml:mn><mml:mtext> rpm</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> for 20 min. The organic phase was collected in a new PP tube after centrifuging at <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i41" display="inline" alttext="6000 revolutions per minute"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>6,000</mml:mn><mml:mtext> rpm</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> for 10 min. The extraction process was repeated, and the organic extracts were combined and then almost dried out under nitrogen. Following that, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i42" display="inline" alttext="5 milliliters"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>5</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of methanol was added to reconstitute the mixture.</p><p>A Cleanert PEP cartridge (Bonna-Agela Technologies) was used to purify the concentrated extract after it had been preconditioned with <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i43" display="inline" alttext="10 milliliters"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>10</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of methanol and <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i44" display="inline" alttext="10 milliliters"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>10</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of water. This process was followed by washing with <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i45" display="inline" alttext="5 milliliters"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>5</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of 20% methanol in water. The target chemicals were pumped dry and then eluted with <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i46" display="inline" alttext="10 milliliters"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>10</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of methanol. Under nitrogen flow, the eluate was evaporated to dryness before being reconstituted in <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i47" display="inline" alttext="0.5 milliliter"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>0.5</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of methanol. After that, samples were filtered for ultra-high performance liquid chromatography–tandem mass spectrometry (UPLC-MS/MS) analysis using a <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i48" display="inline" alttext="0.22 micrometer"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>0.22</mml:mn><mml:mtext>-</mml:mtext><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">m</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> filter. The three target PFAS were analyzed on a Vanquish UPLC system combined with a TSQ Altis quadrupole mass spectrometer (ThermoFisher Scientific) with a BEH C18 column (Table S3).</p></sec><sec id="s2.4" disp-level="2"><title>Quality Assurance and Quality Control</title><p>PFAS were quantified using isotopically labeled standards (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i49" display="inline" alttext="2 nanograms per milliliter"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mtext> ng</mml:mtext><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>). The average recovery of PFAS in the spiked samples was <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i50" display="inline" alttext="103 plus or minus 15 percent"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>103</mml:mn><mml:mo>±</mml:mo><mml:mn>6.15</mml:mn><mml:mo>%</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> (Table S4). Thus, the concentrations were reported directly without correction by recoveries. Interday and intraday precision and accuracy of the assay were determined by analyzing three quality control samples on the same day (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i51" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic n equals 6"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>6</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) and three consecutive days (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i52" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic n equals 6"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>6</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>). The recovery of intraday and interday injections ranged from 94.8% to 104.6% and 92.6% to 102%, respectively. The coefficients of variation of intraday and interday recovery were 3.60% and 3.78%.</p><p>Duplicate analysis was included for every 10 samples, and the average relative standard deviation of the duplicate analysis was 3.70%. A procedure blank and one solvent blank (HPLC grade methanol) were carried out after 10 samples to monitor for any background contamination and instrument carryover. No target compounds were observed in the procedure blanks or solvent blanks. Blind duplicate samples and standards were analyzed for each batch of 10 samples to examine the stability of the instrument. The method detection limit is shown in Table S5.</p></sec><sec id="s2.5" disp-level="2"><title>PBTK Modeling</title><sec id="s2.5.1" disp-level="3"><title>Description of the route-specific PBTK models.</title><p>Based on the PBTK models for prediction of PFAS in mice, rats, monkeys, and humans reported in literatures,<sup><xref rid="c24" ref-type="bibr">24</xref>,<xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref></sup> the PBTK models via the individual exposure routes in this study consisted of compartments representing plasma, liver, kidney, lung, dermis, and the rest of the body (<xref rid="f1" ref-type="fig">Figure 1</xref>). Only stomach, intestines, and chamber were included in the oral and dermal exposure groups, respectively. Lung and skin were merely considered as the storage compartments in the nonnasal and nondermal routes, respectively, where no absorption, metabolism or elimination occurred. All the tissues are perfusion limited and well mixed.<sup><xref rid="c39" ref-type="bibr">39</xref></sup> Biotransformation was not included because of the strong stability of PFHxS, PFOS, and PFOA in organisms.<sup><xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup> Lung and dermis served as a storage compartment for nonnasal and nondermal exposure, respectively. It is acknowledged that PFOA and PFOS are strongly bound to proteins in plasma such as albumin in mammals.<sup><xref rid="c40" ref-type="bibr">40</xref></sup> Han et al. reported that <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i53" display="inline" alttext="greater than 90 percent"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&gt;</mml:mo><mml:mn>90</mml:mn><mml:mo>%</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of PFOA was bound to serum albumin in rat and human blood.<sup><xref rid="c40" ref-type="bibr">40</xref></sup> The thermodynamic model assumes that only the free fraction of a chemical is pharmacologically active,<sup><xref rid="c41" ref-type="bibr">41</xref></sup> which is supported by the fact that the PBTK models based on the free fraction of PFAS usually generated the best fit of the plasma and tissue concentrations.<sup><xref rid="c42" ref-type="bibr">42</xref>,<xref rid="c43" ref-type="bibr">43</xref></sup> In this study, the initial value (0.09) of the free fraction was taken from Chou et al.<sup><xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref></sup> The vascular and extravascular areas are thought to be well stirred with quick equilibrium in the flow-limited model. The venous concentration of a chemical leaving a tissue is equal to its free concentration in the tissue, which is calculated as the total concentration in the tissue multiplied by the quotient of the free fraction and tissue-specific partition coefficient.</p><fig id="f1" position="float"><?disp-level 4?><label>Figure 1.</label><caption><p>Structures of the PBTK models for PFAS in the mouse following oral (A), nasal (B), dermal (C), and IV exposure. They were modified based on Chou and Lin<sup><xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref></sup> and Loccisano et al.<sup><xref rid="c24" ref-type="bibr">24</xref></sup> Note: IV, intravenous injection; <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i54" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript 0 uppercase c end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">C</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, rate of uptake from stomach into the liver, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i55" display="inline" alttext="1 per open parenthesis hour asterisk body weight begin superscript negative 0.25 end superscript close parenthesis"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">h</mml:mi><mml:mo>×</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>BW</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:mn>0.25</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>; <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i56" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript absorbed elimination rate constant end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>absC</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, rate of absorption of chemical from intestine to liver, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i57" display="inline" alttext="1 per open parenthesis hour asterisk body weight begin superscript negative 0.25 end superscript close parenthesis"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">h</mml:mi><mml:mo>×</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>BW</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:mn>0.25</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>; <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i58" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript bile elimination rate constant end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>bileC</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, bile elimination rate constant, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i59" display="inline" alttext="1 per open parenthesis hour asterisk body weight begin superscript negative 0.25 end superscript close parenthesis"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">h</mml:mi><mml:mo>×</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>BW</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:mn>0.25</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>; <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i67" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript lowercase p end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">p</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, the permeability coefficients for dermis, cm/h; Step 1: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the lung has a unidirectional relationship with the lung. Step 2: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the skin has a unidirectional relationship with the skin. Step 3: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the rest of the body has a bidirectional relationship with the rest of the body. Step 4: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the liver has a bidirectional relationship with the liver. Step 5: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the kidney has a bidirectional relationship with the kidney. Step 6: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the filtrate has a unidirectional relationship with the filtrate. Step 7: Oral leads to stomach. Step 8: Stomach with the rate of uptake from the stomach into the liver leads to the liver and intestines. Step 9: Intestine with the rate of absorption of chemical from intestine to liver leads to liver, and the rate of unabsorbed dose leads to storage. Step 10: Liver with a constant bile elimination rate leads to storage. Step 11: Filtrate with maximal reabsorption rate and affinity constant lead to kidney. Step 12: Filtrate with a urine elimination rate constant, leading to urine. Figure 1(B) is a schematic flowchart titled Nasal with eleven steps. Step 1: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the lung has a bidirectional relationship with the lung. Step 2: Lung leads to the end exhaled breath with alveolar ventilation rate and lung-to-end exhaled breath partition coefficient. Step 3: Nasal leads to lung. Step 4: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the skin has a bidirectional relationship with the skin. Step 5: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the rest of the body has a bidirectional relationship with the rest of the body. Step 6: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the liver has a bidirectional relationship with the liver. Step 7: A liver with a constant rate of bile elimination leads to storage. Step 8: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the kidney has a bidirectional relationship with the kidney. Step 9: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the filtrate has a unidirectional relationship with the filtrate. Step 10: Filtrate with maximal reabsorption rate and affinity constant lead to kidney. Step 11: Filtrate at a constant rate of urine elimination. Figure 1(C) is a schematic flowchart titled Dermal with twelve steps. Step 1: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the lung has a bidirectional relationship with the lung. Step 2: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the dermis has a bidirectional relationship with the dermis. Step 3: Dermis with the permeability coefficients for dermis, exposure surface area, and dermis-to-chamber partition coefficent has a bidirectional relationship with the Chamber. Step 4: Chamber with loss rate from chamber leads to loss. Step 5: Dermal leads to chamber. Step 6: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the rest of the body has a bidirectional relationship with the rest of the body. Step 7: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the liver has a bidirectional relationship with the liver. Step 8: Liver with bile elimination rate constant leads to storage. Step 9: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the kidney has a bidirectional relationship with the kidney. Step 10: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the filtrate has a unidirectional relationship with the filtrate. Step 11: Filtrate with maximal reabsorption rate and affinity constant lead to kidney. Step 12: Filtrate at a constant rate of urine elimination. Figure 1(D) is a schematic flowchart titled intravenous injection with ten steps. Step 1: Intravenous injection lead to plasma. Step 2: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the lung has a bidirectional relationship with the lung. Step 3: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the skin has a bidirectional relationship with the skin. Step 4: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the rest of the body has a bidirectional relationship with the rest of the body. Step 5: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the liver has a bidirectional relationship with the liver. Step 6: Liver with bile elimination rate constant lead to storage. Step 7: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the kidney has a bidirectional relationship with the kidney. Step 8: Plasma with fractional blood flow to the filtrate has a unidirectional relationship with the filtrate. Step 9: Filtrate with maximal reabsorption rate and affinity constant lead to kidney. Step 10: Filtrate at a constant rate of urine elimination.</alt-text><alternatives><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="image" xlink:href="ehp11969_f1.jpg"><?cloudpmc-path blobs/4169/10718298/0b34da679001/ehp11969_f1.jpg?><?cloudpmc-bucket cdn?><?image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?original-height 2396?><?original-width 2400?><?scaled-height 781?><?scaled-width 782?></graphic><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="thumb" xlink:href="ehp11969_f1.gif"><?cloudpmc-path blobs/4169/10718298/40020e6ca28c/ehp11969_f1.gif?><?cloudpmc-bucket cdn?></graphic></alternatives></fig><p>The oral absorption of PFAS was described in a two-compartment gastrointestinal (GI) model.<sup><xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref></sup> After absorption, PFAS are transported directly from GI tract to the liver via the portal vein. coefficient (unitless).</p><p>In one of our recent studies, PFAS were applied on the skin surface of the rats, and the absorption percentages of PFAS were determined (7.74%, 7.21%, and 8.30% for PFHxS, PFOS, and PFOA, respectively).<sup><xref rid="c31" ref-type="bibr">31</xref></sup> When the dosage was multiplied by the absorption percentage obtained in that study, the model was successful to simulate the concentration of the target PFAS in the rat blood, dermis, and liver (Figure S1). To avoid overestimating the internal level due to intradermal injection, the applied dosage was corrected by the absorption percentage when simulating the pharmacokinetics of dermal exposure. The first-order rate constant (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i104" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript urine end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>K</mml:mtext></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>urine</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, 1/h) was used to describe the urinary excretion rate of PFAS. It has been speculated that renal resorption through transporters in the proximal tubule of the kidney is responsible for the prolonged plasma half-lives of PFAS.<sup><xref rid="c47" ref-type="bibr">47</xref></sup> Thus, the filtrate compartment described the saturable renal resorption with the maximal reabsorption rate (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i105" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t begin subscript lowercase m end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>T</mml:mtext></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">m</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, ng/h) and transporter affinity constant (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i106" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript lowercase t end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>K</mml:mtext></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, ng/mL).<sup><xref rid="c22" ref-type="bibr">22</xref></sup> The MBDEs for the liver, kidney, and filtrate are as follows:

partition coefficient.</p></sec><sec id="s2.5.2" disp-level="3"><title>Parameterization.</title><p>All physiological parameters obtained from literatures were fixed in the model calibration (Table S6).<sup><xref rid="c24" ref-type="bibr">24</xref>,<xref rid="c49" ref-type="bibr">49</xref>–<xref rid="c52" ref-type="bibr">52</xref></sup> The fitted chemical parameters are summarized in Table S7. The parameters of blood flows and maximal reabsorption rate were scaled as body weight <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i128" display="inline" alttext="body weight begin superscript 0.75 end superscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>BW</mml:mtext><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>0.75</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>; the parameters of the first-order rate constants were scaled as <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i129" display="inline" alttext="body weight begin superscript negative 0.25 end superscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>BW</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:mn>0.25</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>; and the partition coefficients, affinity constant, and tissue volumes were scaled linearly to body weight.<sup><xref rid="c53" ref-type="bibr">53</xref></sup> Low- and high-level dosed groups were used to calibrate and validate the PBTK models, respectively. The models were calibrated by fitting the measured concentrations of the target compounds in the plasma, liver, kidney, lung (except nasal route), and skin (except dermal route) of the dosed mice. It was hypothesized that exposure routes did not affect the rates of metabolism and elimination of PFAS.<sup><xref rid="c54" ref-type="bibr">54</xref>,<xref rid="c55" ref-type="bibr">55</xref></sup> Therefore, parameters related to renal resorption, the body weight normalized maximal reabsorption rate (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i130" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t begin subscript lowercase m c end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">T</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>mc</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> and <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i131" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript lowercase t end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) and bile elimination rate constant (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i132" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript bile elimination rate constant end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>bileC</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) were calibrated by the results obtained from IV (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i133" display="inline" alttext="1 milligram per kilogram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>). Additional calibration of the urine elimination rate constant (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i134" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript urine elimination rate constant end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>urine</mml:mtext><mml:mi>C</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) was conducted to fit the amount of PFAS eliminated through urine as shown in previous studies.<sup><xref rid="c28" ref-type="bibr">28</xref>,<xref rid="c29" ref-type="bibr">29</xref>,<xref rid="c56" ref-type="bibr">56</xref></sup> Then they were applied in the calibration of the PBTK models via other routes.<sup><xref rid="c57" ref-type="bibr">57</xref></sup></p><p>A preliminary sensitivity analysis was conducted to identify sensitive parameters followed by calibrations using the Levenberg-Marquardt algorithm.<sup><xref rid="c42" ref-type="bibr">42</xref></sup> The purpose was to reduce the computing load and improve the quality of the performance. The route-specific bioavailability of PFAS was calculated by dividing the predicted area under the concentration curve (AUC) via oral, nasal, or dermal route by the AUC via IV.<sup><xref rid="c58" ref-type="bibr">58</xref></sup> Based on the predicted concentration-time data, the elimination half-life (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i135" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t begin subscript half end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">T</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, d) was calculated as follows<sup><xref rid="c59" ref-type="bibr">59</xref></sup>:
</p><disp-formula id="d10"><label>(10)</label><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="m10" display="block" alttext="uppercase t begin subscript half end subscript equals natural logarithm open parenthesis 2 close parenthesis divided by lowercase k begin subscript lowercase e end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>T</mml:mtext></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mtext>ln</mml:mtext><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mtext>k</mml:mtext></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>e</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></disp-formula><disp-formula <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i136" display="inline" alttext="lowercase k begin subscript lowercase e end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>k</mml:mtext></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>e</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> is the first-order elimination rate (/d), <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i137" display="inline" alttext="uppercase c begin subscript end end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>C</mml:mtext></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>end</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> (ng/mL), and <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i138" display="inline" alttext="uppercase c begin subscript maximum end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>C</mml:mtext></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>max</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> (ng/mL) are the predicted concentration of PFAS at the end (day 40) and the maximum predicted concentration of PFAS in plasma, respectively. <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i139" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t begin subscript end end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>T</mml:mtext></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>end</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> (d) and <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i140" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t begin subscript maximum end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>T</mml:mtext></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>max</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> (d) is the time at the end point of prediction (40 d in this study) and time to reach the peak concentration.</p></sec><sec id="s2.5.3" disp-level="3"><title>Model evaluation.</title><p>By contrasting the predicted concentration-time kinetic profiles with the experimental results, the performance of the PBPK model was assessed.<sup><xref rid="c60" ref-type="bibr">60</xref></sup> If the predicted values were within a factor of two of the observed values, it was regarded as satisfactory.<sup><xref rid="c60" ref-type="bibr">60</xref></sup> The degree of fit between the <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i141" display="inline" alttext="logarithm, base 10"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">log</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>10</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>-transformed observed and predicted values was assessed using linear regression.</p><p>A local sensitivity analysis was carried out by adjusting each parameter by 1% stepwise from the original value. The normalized sensitivity coefficient (NSC) was determined to examine the impact on the area AUC of plasma (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i142" display="inline" alttext="area under the curve begin subscript plasma end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>AUC</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>plasma</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) because it reflects the relative amount of PFAS absorbed into the systemic circulation.<sup><xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref></sup> The equation to calculate NSC is<sup><xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref></sup>:
</p><disp-formula id="d12"><label>(12)</label><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="m12" display="block" alttext="normalized sensitivity coefficient equals open parenthesis uppercase delta lowercase r per lowercase r close parenthesis times open parenthesis lowercase p per uppercase delta lowercase p"><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>NSC</mml:mtext><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Δ</mml:mi><mml:mi>r</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi>r</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>×</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi>p</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Δ</mml:mi><mml:mi>p</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></disp-formula><p>where <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i143" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic r"><mml:mi>r</mml:mi></mml:math></inline-formula> is the response variable; Δ<italic>r</italic> is the change in the response variable caused by 1% increase in the parameter value; <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i144" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p"><mml:mi>p</mml:mi></mml:math></inline-formula> is the parameter’s original value, and Δ<italic>p</italic> is 1% of the parameter’s original value.</p></sec></sec><sec id="s2.6" disp-level="2"><title>Extrapolating from Single- to Repeated-Dose Exposure Scenario</title><p>Because organisms in the environment are usually exposed to pollutants continuously, the PBTK model established from the single-dose scenario was extrapolated to a repeated-dose exposure scenario. We collected the data sets obtained from rat or mice models reported in literature (Table S8) and compared them with the results predicted by the model. The exposure scenarios, body weight, and alveolar ventilation (if necessary) of rats or mice were consistent with those in the literature. In the three oral exposure studies, the exposure duration was 7 d, 42 d, and 104 wk, respectively.<sup><xref rid="c61" ref-type="bibr">61</xref>–<xref rid="c63" ref-type="bibr">63</xref></sup> For nasal and dermal exposure routes, only two studies were available for model evaluation of repeated nasal (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i145" display="inline" alttext="1 milligram per meter cubed"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mtext> </mml:mtext><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">m</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>3</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> PFOA, 6 h/d, 5 d/wk for 3 wk) and dermal exposure (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i146" display="inline" alttext="20 milligrams per kilogram per day"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>20</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">d</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> PFOA for 10 d).<sup><xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref>,<xref rid="c64" ref-type="bibr">64</xref></sup> The original exposure scenario of nasal exposure was adjusted to <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i147" display="inline" alttext="953.7 micrograms per kilogram per week"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>953.7</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">g</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">wk</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> for 30 wk (when steady state was reached) derived from the body weight (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i148" display="inline" alttext="216 grams"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>216</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) and alveolar ventilation rate (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i149" display="inline" alttext="31.74 milliliters per hour per gram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>31.74</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">h</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) of the rat to fit our model.<sup><xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref>,<xref rid="c49" ref-type="bibr">49</xref></sup></p></sec><sec id="s2.7" disp-level="2"><title>Extrapolating from Mouse to Human</title><p>Because oral intake is the main exposure route for general people and there are abundant data on human exposure to PFOS, the established oral PBTK model of PFOS was extrapolated to human assuming that all the estimated daily intake (EDI) enters the human body orally. The physiological parameters of mice were replaced with those of human (Table S9). Haug et al. determined the PFOS concentrations in the same Norway population from 1977 to 2006, which were used for model calibration (Table S10).<sup><xref rid="c65" ref-type="bibr">65</xref></sup> Then, the model was validated with the data set from the United States,<sup><xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup> Canada,<sup><xref rid="c66" ref-type="bibr">66</xref>–<xref rid="c68" ref-type="bibr">68</xref></sup> and Australia populations.<sup><xref rid="c69" ref-type="bibr">69</xref>–<xref rid="c71" ref-type="bibr">71</xref></sup> After that, the nasal and dermal models were extrapolated to human by using the Free, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i150" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript bile elimination rate constant end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>bileC</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, and rest of body-to-plasma partition coefficient (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i151" display="inline" alttext="uppercase p begin subscript rest end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">P</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>Rest</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) value calibrated in the oral human model.</p><p>Poothong et al. determined the EDI values of PFOS through diet and dust digestion, air inhalation, and skin absorption for general people, which were used to estimate the steady-state concentration of PFOS in human blood based on the established human PBTK models.<sup><xref rid="c32" ref-type="bibr">32</xref></sup> The sum of the simulated steady-state concentration of PFOS via each route was considered the total concentration of PFOS in human blood.</p></sec><sec id="s2.8" disp-level="2"><title>Data Analyses</title><p>All model simulations were conducted using R (version 4.1.2; R Development Core Team). The model codes were adapted from the code in a previous study.<sup><xref rid="c42" ref-type="bibr">42</xref></sup> The PBTK model was coded in R package “mrgsolve.” Linear regression analysis and plotting were performed with GraphPad Prism 8 (GraphPad Software, Inc.). The model code is provided in the supplemental file, “Model Codes.txt.”</p></sec></sec><sec id="s3" disp-level="1"><title>Results</title><sec id="s3.1" disp-level="2"><title>Physiological Status of Mice</title><p>No mortality was observed during the experiment. There was no significant difference between the exposed and control groups in the body weight and liver:body weight ratio, which suggests that no distinct significant toxic effect was observed throughout the experiment (Tables S11 and S12). The concentrations of PFHxS, PFOS, and PFOA in the plasma of the control group were about 2–4 orders of magnitude lower than the exposure groups (Table S13; average 4.63, 2.67, and <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i152" display="inline" alttext="6.28 nanograms per milliliter"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>6.28</mml:mn><mml:mtext> ng</mml:mtext><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, respectively), and therefore contamination from background was considered to be negligible. They were all detected in all the target tissues of the test groups, suggesting that they were absorbed into the tissues through the individual specific exposure routes.</p></sec><sec id="s3.2" disp-level="2"><title>Model Evaluation</title><p><xref rid="f2" ref-type="fig">Figure 2</xref> and Figure S2 compare the predicted accumulation kinetics of PFHxS, PFOS, and PFOA using the individual PBTK models with the observed results for the purpose of calibration (dose at <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i153" display="inline" alttext="1 milligram per kilogram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) and validation (dose at <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i154" display="inline" alttext="5 milligrams per kilogram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>5</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>). The route-specific PBTK models calibrated at a dose of <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i155" display="inline" alttext="1 milligram per kilogram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> well simulated the concentration-time curves at a dose of <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i156" display="inline" alttext="5 milligrams per kilogram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>5</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> (Figures S3–S14). Overall, 98.8% and 84.2% of the predicted concentrations of PFAS in the tissues dosed at <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i157" display="inline" alttext="1 milligram per kilogram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> and <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i158" display="inline" alttext="5 milligrams per kilogram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>5</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, respectively, fell within a predicted-to-observed ratio of 2:1. The predicted concentrations of the three PFAS generated from the individual models correlated with the measured ones very well (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i159" display="inline" alttext="uppercase italic r squared equals 0.95 to 0.98"><mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>R</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>0.95</mml:mn><mml:mo>–</mml:mo><mml:mn>0.98</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> for the dosage of <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i160" display="inline" alttext="1 milligram per kilogram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> and <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i161" display="inline" alttext="uppercase italic r squared equals 0.85 to 0.92"><mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>R</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>0.85</mml:mn><mml:mo>–</mml:mo><mml:mn>0.92</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> for the dosage of <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i162" display="inline" alttext="5 milligrams per kilogram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>5</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>). The slopes of the linear fitting equation were very close to 1 (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i163" display="inline" alttext="1.02 plus or minus 0.14"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1.02</mml:mn><mml:mo>±</mml:mo><mml:mn>0.14</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> for the dosage of <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i164" display="inline" alttext="1 milligram per kilogram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> and <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i165" display="inline" alttext="1.11 plus or minus 0.17"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1.11</mml:mn><mml:mo>±</mml:mo><mml:mn>0.17</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> for the dosage of <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i166" display="inline" alttext="5 milligrams per kilogram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>5</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i167" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p less than 0.0001"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>p</mml:mi><mml:mo>&lt;</mml:mo><mml:mn>0.0001</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>), suggesting the established models are reliable. The predicted amount of these three PFAS in urine also fit the results in previous mice studies very well (Figure S15). At the end of the simulation (40 d), the cumulative excretion of PFHxS in urine was the highest (11.7%–24.0% of the dosage), followed by PFOA and PFOS (2.28%–11.15%, and 0.694%–2.04%, respectively) (Figure S16).</p><fig id="f2" position="float"><?disp-level 3?><label>Figure 2.</label><caption><p>An overall evaluation of goodness of model fit for PFAS between the model-predicted and the observed concentration following <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i168" display="inline" alttext="1 milligram per kilogram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> oral (A), nasal (B), dermal (C), and IV (D) exposure. for all PFAS. Gastric and intestinal parameters (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i193" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript 0 uppercase c end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">C</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> and <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i194" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript absorbed elimination rate constant end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>absC</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) had an obvious effect on <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i195" display="inline" alttext="area under the curve begin subscript plasma end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>AUC</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>plasma</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of PFHxS for oral exposure.</p></sec><sec id="s3.3" disp-level="2"><title>Pharmacokinetics and Bioavailability of PFAS in Mice</title><p><xref rid="f3" ref-type="fig">Figure 3A–C</xref> illustrates the simulated concentration-time curves of PFHxS, PFOS, and PFOA in the plasma, respectively. The <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i196" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t begin subscript maximum end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">T</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>max</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of PFAS in plasma (<xref rid="f3" ref-type="fig">Figure 3D</xref>) was the longest in the dermal exposure groups (1.42–3.46 d), followed by the oral (0.708–1.71 d) and nasal exposure groups (0.0417 d). For dermal and oral exposure, the order of <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i197" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t begin subscript maximum end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">T</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>max</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> was PFOS (3.46 d) <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i198" display="inline" alttext="greater than perfluorohexanesulfonic acid"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&gt;</mml:mo><mml:mtext>PFHxS</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> (1.83 d) <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i199" display="inline" alttext="greater than perfluorooctanoic acid"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&gt;</mml:mo><mml:mtext>PFOA</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> (1.42 d), and PFOS (1.71 d) <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i200" display="inline" alttext="greater than perfluorooctanoic acid"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&gt;</mml:mo><mml:mtext>PFOA</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> (1.33 d) <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i201" was negatively associated with the value of <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i202" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript lowercase p end
