The corresponding numeric data are shown in Excel Table S4. Note: Con, concentration.</p></caption><alt-text>Figures 5(A1) and 5(A2) are ribbon plus line graph, plotting concentration in plasma (nanogram per milliliter), ranging from 0 to 6000 in increments of 2000 and concentration in the liver (nanogram per milliliter), ranging from, 0 to 30000 in increments of 10000 (y-axis) across time (day), ranging from 0 to 600 in increments of 200 (x-axis) for 0.024 milligram per kilogram per day, plasma and 0.024 milligram per kilogram per day, liver. Figures 5(B1) and 5(B2) are ribbon plus line graph, plotting concentration in plasma (nanogram per milliliter), ranging from 0 to 25000 in increments of 5000 and concentration in the liver (nanogram per milliliter), ranging as 0e plus 00, 5e plus 04, and 1e plus 05 (y-axis) across time (day), ranging from 0 to 600 in increments of 200 (x-axis) for 0.098 milligram per kilogram per day, plasma and 0.098 milligram per kilogram per day, liver. Figures 5(C1) and 5(C2) are ribbon plus line graph, plotting concentration in plasma (nanogram per milliliter), ranging from 0 to 60000 in increments of 20000 and concentration in the liver (nanogram per milliliter), ranging as 0e plus 00, 1e plus 05, 2e plus 05, and 3e plus 05 (y-axis) across time (day), ranging from 0 to 600 in increments of 200 (x-axis) for 0.242 milligram per kilogram per day, plasma and 0.242 milligram per kilogram per day, liver. Figures 5(D1) and 5(D2) are ribbon plus line graph, plotting concentration in plasma (nanogram per milliliter), ranging from 0 to 250000 in increments of 50000 and concentration in the liver (nanogram per milliliter), ranging as 0e plus 00, 5e plus 05, and 1e plus 06 (y-axis) across time (day), ranging from 0 to 600 in increments of 200 (x-axis) for 0.984 milligram per kilogram per day, plasma and 0.984 milligram per kilogram per day, liver.</alt-text><alternatives><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="image" xlink:href="ehp11969_f5.jpg"><?cloudpmc-path blobs/4169/10718298/6395924bd476/ehp11969_f5.jpg?><?cloudpmc-bucket cdn?><?image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?original-height 3335?><?original-width 2400?><?scaled-height 1101?><?scaled-width 792?></graphic><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="thumb" xlink:href="ehp11969_f5.gif"><?cloudpmc-path blobs/4169/10718298/485285e72dd6/ehp11969_f5.gif?><?cloudpmc-bucket cdn?></graphic></alternatives></fig><p>The results indicate that the human model performed very well in prediction of the PFOS concentration in human blood (<xref rid="f6" ref-type="fig">Figure 6</xref>). The Free, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i234" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript bile elimination rate constant end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>bileC</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, and <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i235" display="inline" alttext="uppercase p begin subscript rest end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">P</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>Rest</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> in the human PBTK model were much lower than that in the mouse model (Table S10). The simulated PFOS concentration in human plasma via separate oral, nasal, and dermal exposure is shown in Figure S24. Oral intake was estimated to make the most contribution to the internal level of PFOS in the human model. It is worth noting that although the daily intake of PFOS through the nasal route (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i236" display="inline" alttext="3.5 picograms per kilogram per day"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>3.5</mml:mn><mml:mtext> pg</mml:mtext><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">d</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) was 18.4 times that of dermal route (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i237" display="inline" alttext="0.19 picogram per kilogram per day"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>0.19</mml:mn><mml:mtext> pg</mml:mtext><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">d</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>), the contribution of the nasal route to the internal level was comparable to that of dermal route (0.0235% and 0.0355%, respectively) (Table S14).</p><fig id="f6" position="float"><?disp-level 3?><label>Figure 6.</label><caption><p>Model calibration (Norway<sup><xref rid="c65" ref-type="bibr">65</xref></sup>) and verification (Australia,<sup><xref rid="c69" ref-type="bibr">69</xref>–<xref rid="c71" ref-type="bibr">71</xref></sup> Canada,<sup><xref rid="c66" ref-type="bibr">66</xref>–<xref rid="c68" ref-type="bibr">68</xref></sup> and United States<sup><xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup>) results for human model of PFOS. It is assumed that all the estimated daily intake entered into the human body through oral route. The shaded area represents a predicted-to-observed ratio between 0.5:1 and 2:1. The corresponding numeric data are shown in Excel Table S5. Note: PFOS, perfluorooctane sulfonate; US, United States.</p></caption><alt-text>Figure 6 is a ribbon plus line graph, plotting concentration (nanogram per milliliter), ranging from 0 to 60 in increments of 20 (y-axis) across year, ranging from 1980 to 2020 in increments of 10 years (x-axis) for Australia, Canada, United States, and Norway.</alt-text><alternatives><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="image" xlink:href="ehp11969_f6.jpg"><?cloudpmc-path blobs/4169/10718298/8106e114b289/ehp11969_f6.jpg?><?cloudpmc-bucket cdn?><?image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?original-height 1763?><?original-width 2400?><?scaled-height 592?><?scaled-width 806?></graphic><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="thumb" xlink:href="ehp11969_f6.gif"><?cloudpmc-path blobs/4169/10718298/577e615a5f46/ehp11969_f6.gif?><?cloudpmc-bucket cdn?></graphic></alternatives></fig></sec></sec><sec id="s4" disp-level="1"><title>Discussion</title><p>In this study, mice were exposed to PFAS via oral, nasal, dermal exposure routes singly or jointly. The route-specific PBTK models were established to investigate the route-specific effects on the pharmacokinetics of PFHxS, PFOS, and PFOA. For the first time to our knowledge, nasal and dermal routes were included in the PBTK models of PFAS. These PBTK models successfully simulated the pharmacokinetics of PFAS in mice after low- and high-level exposures. The results suggest that exposure routes significantly affect the uptake of PFAS in mice, resulting in the highest bioavailability of PFAS after oral exposure, followed by nasal and dermal exposure. The combined model of the three exposure routes performed well to describe the accumulation of PFAS via concurrent multiple-route exposure, which happens in realistic environments. The models were also powerful to predict the concentrations of PFAS in the plasma and various tissues of rodents and humans under repeated exposure in long-term situations.</p><sec id="s4.1" disp-level="2"><title>Route-Specific PBTK Model for PFAS</title><p>As shown in <xref rid="f2" ref-type="fig">Figures 2</xref> and Figure S2, the simulated data obtained from the four individual PBTK models correlated with the observed data very well, which meet the World Health Organization (WHO) model precision criteria,<sup><xref rid="c60" ref-type="bibr">60</xref></sup> confirming that the established route-specific PBTK models were capable of simulating the single-dose exposure of PFHxS, PFOS, and PFOA in mice. Deviations between the predicted and measured values at a few time points might be attributed to the dose-dependent pharmacokinetic process of PFAS <italic>in vivo</italic> and changes in the physiological parameters of the mice over time.<sup><xref rid="c42" ref-type="bibr">42</xref></sup> The models were used to predict the PFAS concentrations in the serum of mice dosed at <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i238" display="inline" alttext="1 milligram per kilogram"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mg</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> by Lou et al.,<sup><xref rid="c72" ref-type="bibr">72</xref></sup> Chang et al.,<sup><xref rid="c28" ref-type="bibr">28</xref></sup> and Sundstrom et al.<sup><xref rid="c29" ref-type="bibr">29</xref></sup> The model well predicted the <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i239" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t begin subscript maximum end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">T</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>max</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, though it slightly underestimated the observed concentrations in serum (Figure S25). This slight underestimation might be attributed to the mixed PFAS exposure used in this study, leading to competition among the PFAS with different carbon chain lengths and structures when binding to the limited protein cavity sites.<sup><xref rid="c73" ref-type="bibr">73</xref></sup> Similarly, the weaker binding affinity of PFOA to liver fatty acid binding protein (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i240" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript lowercase d-uppercase l p end subscript equals 197 plus or minus 13"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">d</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mtext>-LP</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>197</mml:mn><mml:mo>±</mml:mo><mml:mn>13</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) than PFOS (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i241" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript lowercase d-uppercase l p end subscript equals 81 plus or minus 7"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">d</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mtext>-LP</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>81</mml:mn><mml:mo>±</mml:mo><mml:mn>7</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) may account for its lower PL under mixed PFAS exposure in this study than that exposed to PFOA alone in previous studies.<sup><xref rid="c72" ref-type="bibr">72</xref>,<xref rid="c74" ref-type="bibr">74</xref>,<xref rid="c75" ref-type="bibr">75</xref></sup></p><p><inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i242" display="inline" alttext="area under the curve begin subscript plasma end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>AUC</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>plasma</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> is a dose-dependent parameter. BW and Free were the most sensitive parameters for <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i243" display="inline" alttext="area under the curve begin subscript plasma end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>AUC</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>plasma</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of target PFAS and determined the dose and the amount absorbed into the systemic circulation, respectively. The estimated Free (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i244" display="inline" alttext="less than 10 percent"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&lt;</mml:mo><mml:mn>10</mml:mn><mml:mo>%</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) was comparable to the value obtained in previous studies.<sup><xref rid="c28" ref-type="bibr">28</xref>,<xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref>,<xref rid="c72" ref-type="bibr">72</xref></sup> In the present study, all the PFAS were sensitive to the distribution and excretion process through the liver. Previous PBTK models were mainly applied to PFOS, which also found that Free, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i245" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript bile elimination rate constant end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>bileC</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, and <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i246" display="inline" alttext="uppercase p begin subscript uppercase l end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">P</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> were key parameters affecting the PFOS concentrations in plasma and tissues in rodents.<sup><xref rid="c42" ref-type="bibr">42</xref></sup> Renal excretion and reabsorption played a decisive role for PFHxS, which might be because PFHxS prefers to partition in plasma.<sup><xref rid="c29" ref-type="bibr">29</xref></sup> This finding suggests that the models were effective in describing the partition behaviors of PFAS with different physicochemical properties.</p></sec><sec id="s4.2" disp-level="2"><title>Differences in Pharmacokinetics and Bioavailability of PFAS</title><p>A distinct hysteretic effect in the plasma was observed in the dermal exposure group (<xref rid="f3" ref-type="fig">Figure 3A–C</xref>), which had also been observed for other organic pollutants such as chlorpyrifos (CPF), polybrominated diphenyl ethers, bisphenol A, and methyl tertiary-butyl ether.<sup><xref rid="c76" ref-type="bibr">76</xref>–<xref rid="c79" ref-type="bibr">79</xref></sup> For example, it was modeled to take 3.79 d for PFOS in the chamber to decrease below 1% of the given dose after dermal exposure, whereas it took only 0.75 and 0.04 d in the stomach and lung after oral and nasal exposure, respectively (Figure S26). This finding suggests that the dermis acts as a barrier to depress the penetration of PFAS; thus, it took a prolonged period for them to be absorbed into the bloodstream. Continuously slow release of contaminants at the sites of dermal absorption could potentially lead to drastically different biological responses than would be expected following other routes. For example, subcutaneous administration of CPF in the rats greatly prolonged inhibition of cholinesterase activity in the brain relative to oral exposure.<sup><xref rid="c80" ref-type="bibr">80</xref></sup> It was reported that the rate at which pollutants were absorbed through the skin into the blood was limited by the penetration rate.<sup><xref rid="c81" ref-type="bibr">81</xref></sup> After percutaneous exposure, the <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i247" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t begin subscript maximum end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">T</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>max</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of PFOS in plasma was greater than that of PFOA, which may be attributed to the weaker permeability and lower volatility of PFOS (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i248" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript lowercase p end subscript equals 1.33 times 10 begin superscript negative 5 end superscript centimeter per hour"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">p</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1.33</mml:mn><mml:mo>×</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>10</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:mn>5</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msup><mml:mtext> </mml:mtext><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">cm</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">h</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i249" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript volume end subscript equals 3.23 times 10 begin superscript negative 5 end superscript per hour"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>vol</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>3.23</mml:mn><mml:mo>×</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>10</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:mn>7</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msup><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">h</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) than PFOA (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i250" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript lowercase p end subscript equals 1.47 times 10 begin superscript negative 3 end superscript centimeter per hour"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">p</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1.47</mml:mn><mml:mo>×</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>10</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:mn>3</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msup><mml:mtext> </mml:mtext><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">cm</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">h</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i251" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript volume end subscript equals 6.99 times 10 begin superscript negative 7 end superscript per hour"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>vol</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>6.99</mml:mn><mml:mo>×</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>10</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:mn>7</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msup><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">h</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>). PFOS is more hydrophobic and has a larger molecular volume; thus, it was more difficult to pass through the water-rich dermis containing living cells.<sup><xref rid="c82" ref-type="bibr">82</xref></sup></p><p>The <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i252" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t begin subscript maximum end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">T</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>max</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of PFAS in blood was the highest after nasal exposure (0.96 h), which was influenced by the physiological structure of the nasal cavity. The olfactory epithelium comprises almost 50% of the total nasal surface area, which would promote the delivery of PFAS to the olfactory bulb,<sup><xref rid="c83" ref-type="bibr">83</xref></sup> and the abundant capillaries of alveolar epithelial cells expedite the rapid transfer of compounds to blood.<sup><xref rid="c84" ref-type="bibr">84</xref></sup> In addition, anion surfactants were confirmed to be able to promote the nasal absorption of a compound by changing the membrane potential of the mucosa and damaging the close connection between epithelial cells.<sup><xref rid="c85" ref-type="bibr">85</xref></sup> PFAS as anionic surfactants may also be absorbed by the nasal cavity rapidly through these mechanisms. It was modeled that nasal absorption was rapid for many hydrophobic organic pollutants, such as naphthalene, which was independent of storage or metabolism in lung.<sup><xref rid="c86" ref-type="bibr">86</xref></sup> However, this model does not represent absorption of PFAS through the nasal mucosa and upper airways, which may underestimate the absorption rate and proportion of PFAS.</p><p>The elimination rate of PFOA was higher than that of PFOS and PFHxS (<xref rid="f3" ref-type="fig">Figure 3F</xref>). The predicted <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i253" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t begin subscript half end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">T</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> was close to that reported in previous studies using mouse models (30.5–42.8 d for PFOS, 24.9–26.8 d for PFHxS, and 14.7–24.1 for PFOA) and followed the same order in male rats (7.46–82 d for PFOS, 15.9–28.7 d for PFHxS, 1.83–13.4 for PFOA).<sup><xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref>,<xref rid="c72" ref-type="bibr">72</xref></sup> It was predicted that renal reabsorption played an important role in their elimination because the <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i254" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t begin subscript half end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">T</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> was associated with <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i255" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t begin subscript lowercase m c end subscript per uppercase k begin subscript lowercase t end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">T</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>mc</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> for these three PFAS. It was reported that PFAS were excreted by organic anion transporters (Oat1 and Oat3) and reabsorbed by an organic anion transporting polypeptide (Oatp1a1) in the rats.<sup><xref rid="c87" ref-type="bibr">87</xref></sup> Therefore, the substance-specific reabsorption of PFAS may be related to the different binding abilities with the renal transport proteins.</p><p>The modeled results showed that among the three exposure routes, the bioavailability of PFAS decreased following the order of oral <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i256" display="inline" alttext="greater than nasal"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&gt;</mml:mo><mml:mtext>nasal</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>
<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i257" display="inline" alttext="greater than dermal"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&gt;</mml:mo><mml:mtext>dermal</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> route. A similar order was observed for 2,2′,4,4′-tetrabromodiphenyl ether.<sup><xref rid="c76" ref-type="bibr">76</xref></sup> The route-specific <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i258" display="inline" alttext="uppercase c begin subscript maximum end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">C</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>max</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> is a key factor in determining the AUC and therefore bioavailability (<xref rid="f3" ref-type="fig">Figure 3H</xref>). The highest bioavailability via oral exposure indicated that GI was most efficient of the three routes in absorption of PFAS accompanied by the increased proportions in the blood and tissues (Figures S26A, S27A, and S28A). In comparison with oral exposure, PFAS absorbed via nasal exposure might be subjected to exhalation clearance, thus reducing the final bioavailability of PFAS via this route. Despite the low volatility of PFAS due to their ionic nature, the model predicted that 52.6%, 68.9%, and 38.6% of single-dose PFHxS, PFOS, and PFOA, respectively, were excreted by exhalation after 40 d, suggesting that perfluorosulfonate acids (PFSA) had higher exhalation rate than their carboxylic acids (PFCA) counterparts (Figures S26B, S27B, and S28B). During exhalation, a large number of bioaerosol particles with large specific surface areas are produced.<sup><xref rid="c88" ref-type="bibr">88</xref></sup> PFSA were shown to have strong binding affinities with bioaerosol particles, promoting their exhalation and reduced the overall bioavailability.<sup><xref rid="c89" ref-type="bibr">89</xref></sup> A transport carrier such as this is absent in the dermal exposure<sup><xref rid="c90" ref-type="bibr">90</xref></sup>; thus, the predicted loss of PFAS through secreted sebum was relatively low at the end of dermal exposure, which was 12.6% and 2.70% for PFOS and PFHxS, respectively, and PFOA was not eliminated in this way. Combining these results, PFAS due to dermal exposure displayed the lowest bioavailability among the three routes because of their interception by the epidermis. It was reported that hydrophobic compounds are directly delivered into the lipid or protein pool of tissues after oral exposure in a way similar to IV injection, where binding to proteins depresses their removal.<sup><xref rid="c84" ref-type="bibr">84</xref></sup> However, for nasal and dermal exposure, more PFAS exist in free form, and their absorption is mainly via passive diffusion driven by the concentration gradient, favoring interception within the skin layers or exhalation.<sup><xref rid="c84" ref-type="bibr">84</xref></sup></p><p>As for the joint exposure via oral, nasal, and dermal pathways simultaneously, for the first time to our knowledge, we report that there was no synergy or antagonism on the accumulation of PFAS in the tissues among different exposure routes. This finding allowed us to evaluate the relative contribution of each route to the internal doses of PFAS. The combined model was capable of predicting the joint exposure results successfully, thus providing a powerful platform to predict the internal body burdens of PFAS due to concomitant multiroute exposure.</p></sec><sec id="s4.3" disp-level="2"><title>Evaluation of the Repeated Exposure of PFAS</title><p>In general, the models successfully simulated a majority of the observed data derived from short-, medium-, and long-term repeated dose scenarios via different exposure routes. For the repeated oral and nasal exposures, there was a relatively large error between the predicted and observed values at a few time points, which might result from dynamic changes in physiological parameters and concentration dependence of pharmacokinetics. For the case of 104-wk daily oral exposure followed by 1 wk of clearance, the model underestimated the PFOS concentrations in the rat plasma or liver at week 14, whereas it overestimated at week 105 (<xref rid="f5" ref-type="fig">Figure 5</xref>). At the early stage (week 14) following PFOS exposure, Butenhoff et al.<sup><xref rid="c63" ref-type="bibr">63</xref></sup> demonstrated that rats were in a healthy physiological state, and thus the gastrointestinal absorption capacity might be stronger than predicted. At 105 wk after exposure, Butenhoff et al.<sup><xref rid="c63" ref-type="bibr">63</xref></sup> observed significant toxic effects, such as hepatomegaly, and increased excretion of PFOS resulting from the exacerbation of chronic progressive nephritis. In addition, Butenhoff et al.<sup><xref rid="c63" ref-type="bibr">63</xref></sup> observed that the decrease in food intake for the rat at later exposure stage also contributed to the overestimation.</p><p>The model successfully predicted the concentration of PFOA in the rat plasma after repeated nasal exposure, whereas it overestimated the time to reach equilibrium (Figure S22). This overestimation may be due to the uncertainty in the values of physiological parameters such as alveolar ventilation and the body weight of the rats.</p><p>In comparison with the mice PBTK models, much lower Free, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i259" display="inline" alttext="uppercase k begin subscript bile elimination rate constant end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>bileC</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, and <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i260" display="inline" alttext="uppercase p begin subscript rest end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">P</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>Rest</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> values calibrated in the human models well explains the much longer half-life of PFOS in humans reported previously (3.3–27 y).<sup><xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup> The difference between the route-specific external and internal exposures suggest that the relative contribution to the internal exposure levels is relevant to the exposure routes. For people who are exposed to PFAS through the dermal route frequently, considering the absorption efficiency of PFAS would generate a more accurate assessment of exposure risk.</p><p>Although the PBTK model here can be applied to humans, more experiment data are necessary to optimize the human PBTK model and identify the differences in the route-specific absorption processes between human and mice. For example, studies have shown that human skin is considered to be less permeable than mouse skin.<sup><xref rid="c91" ref-type="bibr">91</xref></sup> The airways of mice are directly connected to the alveoli, whereas additional respiratory bronchioles are present in humans,<sup><xref rid="c92" ref-type="bibr">92</xref></sup> which may prolong the transition time in the respiratory airways. In addition, the characteristics of binding to proteins, such as the number of binding sites, dissociation constants, and a time-dependent description of the Free, need to be characterized in detail.<sup><xref rid="c22" ref-type="bibr">22</xref>,<xref rid="c24" ref-type="bibr">24</xref></sup></p><p>In conclusion, this study explored the oral, nasal, and dermal exposure of PFAS simultaneously and clarified the route-specific pharmacokinetics of PFAS in mice. Based on the experimental results, route-specific PBTK models were established for, to our knowledge, the first time to predict the concentrations of PFAS in plasma and tissues following single or joint exposure as well as long-term repeated-dose scenarios. Oral exposure exhibited the highest bioavailability, likely due to a quick and effective absorption. However, dermal and nasal exposures exhibited lower PFAS bioavailability, perhaps due to a distinct hysteresis effect in dermal exposure and clearance via exhalation following nasal exposure. The model was capable of predicting long-term repeated exposure in mice, which was further extrapolated to humans. For people with high nasal and dermal exposure to PFAS, such as firefighters and infants, the contribution of nasal and dermal exposure cannot be excluded from the perspective of long-term exposure. The results improve our understanding of the impacts of exposure routes on ADME kinetics of PFAS in rodents and humans. More studies on nasal and dermal exposure to PFAS are urgently needed to reduce parameter uncertainty. The model would be improved if a full description of the variance in the parameters over time is incorporated. Finally, it is necessary to consider the influence mechanisms of these exposure routes, such as the parameterization related to transmembrane transport, enterohepatic circulation, and protein binding.</p></sec></sec><sec id="sec28" disp-level="1"><title>Supplementary Material</title><supplementary-material id="supplementary-material1" position="float"><media
